23	  **The framing.** The assumption is that a B-rep face boundary is always a
24	  chain of mesh edges, tangent junctions included. There are two ways to use
25	  it, with very different risk: as a **constraint** ("the answer's boundaries
26	  must be mesh-edge chains" — provenance-independent, true for every faceter)
27	  or as a **detector** ("here is where the seams are" — provenance-dependent,
28	  weak on OCCT's Delaunay interiors, information-free on mesh-native input).
29	  **Stage 2 currently uses it as neither.** Stage 3 uses it fully, and is the
30	  proof of concept: §3.x "shared-edge topological assembly instead of geometric
31	  sewing" *deleted* a heuristic-laden alternative outright.
32	
33	  **The prize, quantified.** Almost every tuned parameter in stage 2 is the
34	  price of not knowing the tangent seams. If the partition were known, each
35	  region goes to the whole-region DOF ladder (\`classify_one_region\`, which
36	  already exists and whose few constants are cost-control — subsampling, refit
37	  rounds — not decision thresholds). The evidence is already in hand: **sharp-
38	  edged models pass the ladder with 0 competition regions and no tuning at
39	  all.** Everything below exists only because tangent seams are invisible:
40	  \`SEED_PATCH_CAP\`, \`MAX_PATCH_DEPTH\`, \`PATCH_RADII\`, \`PATCH_TRIM_ROUNDS\`,
41	  \`MIN_CONDITION_FACES\`, \`MAX_GROW_ROUNDS\`, \`GROW_VTOL_FACTOR\`,
42	  \`RESIDUAL_SIGMAS\`, \`MIN_AXIAL_SWEEP_DEG\`, \`MAX_TORUS_RADIUS_RATIO\`,
43	  \`BEND_PATCH_DEPTH\`, \`BEND_PATCH_CAP\`, \`MAX_TORUS_STARTS\`,
44	  \`LEFTOVER_BLOB_MAX\`, \`MERGE_MAX_SMALL\`, \`SMALL_SELECTION_ABSORB_FACES\`,
45	  relabel's 2× margin and 10-round cap, and the freeform island rules —
46	  roughly **two-thirds of stage 2's behavioural thresholds**.
47	
48	  ---
49	
50	  **A. Snap shared region boundaries to mesh-edge chains.** *Start here:
51	  narrow, provenance-independent, and it removes a heuristic rather than adding
52	  one.*
53	
54	  Every remaining \`--compare\` failure has been a boundary sitting one
55	  tessellation row off, and nothing in the pipeline reasons about where a
56	  boundary *should* be — it falls out of flood plus claim resolution. The
57	  assumption guarantees the correct boundary **exists** as a mesh-edge chain,
58	  so this is selection from a guaranteed-nonempty set, not a search.
59	
60	  - Once two adjacent surfaces are committed, compute the curve where they
61	    meet, then reassign the boundary faces by which side of it they fall on,
62	    choosing the mesh-edge chain that best follows it.
63	  - **The hard case is exactly the one that matters.** At a *tangent* junction
64	    the surfaces graze rather than cross, so \`GeomAPI_IntSS\` is the wrong tool
65	    — it is numerically fragile there and that fragility is what produced the
66	    original 4.4e-2 mm error (a plane offset δ moves its intersection with a
67	    torus it should graze by √(2rδ), so tiny fit error is hugely amplified).
68	    For tangency, find the locus of minimum distance between the two fitted
69	    surfaces instead — for a plane grazing a torus that is the tangency circle,
70	    computable in closed form from the fitted parameters. Consider handling
71	    tangent and transversal junctions by different code paths; stage 3.2
72	    already classifies edges as tangent, and stage 3's compare already exempts
73	    tangent edges to the chordal tolerance for this reason.
74	  - Note OCCT gotchas already recorded in DONE/DEVELOPMENT_PLAN: \`IntSS\`
75	    construction can *throw* (knots too close) rather than report \`!IsDone\`.
76	  - **Retires:** relabel's 2× anti-flapping margin and 10-round cap, probably
77	    \`MERGE_MAX_SMALL\`.
78	  - **Validation:** \`stepped_block_toroidal_fillets_medium\` is the target (a
79	    1.6e-1 mm reference-edge compare, \`#[ignore]\`d in stage4_integration);
80	    fine must stay passing. Use \`tmp/probe.sh\` first, full suite to decide.
81	
82	  ---
83	
84	  **B. Estimate \`--surface-tolerance\` and \`--angular-tolerance\` from the mesh
85	  instead of defaulting them.** Both are stand-ins for settings the *exporter*
86	  used and then discarded; neither is a property of the fitting problem.
87	  \`--vertex-tolerance\` is different — it is a genuine precision request from
88	  the user and should stay a plain flag.
89	
90	  *Why it is measurable without a reference STEP.* The mesh is its own
91	  reference, because **vertices lie on the true surface (~1e-6 mm) while
92	  centroids sit inside it by the chordal sag**. For a facet whose vertices lie
93	  on a surface of radius R, with circumradius ρ perpendicular to the axis:
94	  \`depth = R − √(R² − ρ²) ≈ ρ²/(2R)\`. The tessellator sized the facet so this
95	  stays under its deflection setting ε, so the sag distribution over the mesh
96	  *is* a sample of ε.
97	
98	  *How far off the default is:* stage 1's \`--compare\` already reports the true
99	  sag — **8.9e-4 mm** on stepped_block fine, **9.5e-4 mm** on rounded_cube
100	  fine — against a default of **0.4 mm**, i.e. ~400× too loose. (0.4 was a hand
101	  edit in March 2026 with no recorded rationale; see the tolerance-history
102	  notes.) Earlier measurements: worst centroid sag 0.026 mm Onshape-coarse,
103	  0.038 mm Fusion-low.
104	
105	  Three estimators, increasingly independent:
106	  1. **Fit-based, bootstrapped from the unambiguous regions.** Sharp-bounded
107	     regions are solved by the ladder with no tolerance choices beyond vertex
108	     validation. For each of their facets, distance from centroid to its own
109	     committed fit is a direct sag sample. High quantile → ε. Available before
110	     competition runs.
111	  2. **Fit-free, from the mesh's second-order structure.** For adjacent facets
112	     on a cylinder of radius R spanning dihedral θ with width h = Rθ:
113	     \`sag ≈ (h/2)²/(2R) = h²/(8R) = h·θ/8\`. So **sag ≈ dihedral × facet size /
114	     8**, per facet, no fits and no jet inversion — computable in stage 2.1.
115	  3. **Cross-check.** (1) and (2) should agree. Disagreement means the
116	     provenance premise is violated — a decimated, repaired or remeshed STL —
117	     which is itself worth detecting and reporting.
118	
119	  *Prove it before trusting it:* the corpus has reference STEPs and stage 1
120	  \`--compare\` reports the true centroid max distance, so run the reference-free
121	  estimator across the corpus and check it reproduces those numbers. The
122	  reference is the **validation instrument, never the production input**.
123	
124	  *The angular tolerance falls out of the same reasoning.* Its own doc comment
125	  admits it is a stand-in ("Medium-resolution CAD exports step small-radius
126	  fillets up to ~24° between rows… the default must admit those"), and Onshape
127	  exposes the setting directly (12.5° / 6.25° / 2.5° for coarse/medium/fine).
128	  Recover it as a **clustering** rather than a threshold: the dihedral
129	  distribution should be bimodal — tessellation steps near zero, real sharp
130	  edges well separated — so find the gap (Otsu-style). Plot it before
131	  believing it; on stepped_block fine only 384 of 51603 edges are called sharp,
132	  so the populations are very lopsided. **Bonus:** the absence of a clean gap,
133	  or a spiky constant-dihedral distribution, is exactly the OpenSCAD
134	  faceted-by-construction detector described in the item below.
135	
136	  *Risks to measure first:*
137	  - ε is not truly global — exporters also cap angular deviation and minimum
138	    facet width, so different features are bound by different constraints. Take
139	    a high quantile or max, **never a median**, or legitimate faces get
140	    rejected.
141	  - Tightening 0.4 → ~1e-3 is a 400× change that moves behaviour everywhere.
142	    \`REFIT_GIVE_UP\` is defined as 8×stol; merge's centroid gate and every
143	    \`face_validates\` use it. Do the sweep harness (item 6 below) first.
144	  - Coarse meshes genuinely have larger sag — the corpus's coarse variants are
145	    the real test, not the fine ones.
146

211	  *Deliberately not recommended:* seam **detection** proper (finding tangent
212	  seams directly). Curvature-based detection was measured dead in the
213	  2026-07-20 pivot — no threshold separates real junctions from real curvature

219	- **Faceted-by-construction sources (OpenSCAD, Tinkercad, Blender): a flag or
220	  auto-detection.** Not a bug today, and not being worried about yet — recorded
221	  so the eventual decision is informed. These have no B-rep at any stage, so

227	  - \`--angular-tolerance\` (default 25°) silently decides whether an OpenSCAD
228	    revolution comes out as an analytic surface or as the prism it literally
229	    is: \`$fn ≥ 15\` reads smooth, \`$fn ≤ 14\` reads sharp. On OpenSCAD's own
230	    defaults that is radius ≳ 4.5 mm. A \`$fn = 6\` hex nut is handled correctly

232	  - The ambiguity is *genuine* — a 15-gon prism and a coarse cylinder are the

236	  - Expected failure mode where a fit *is* attempted: large radii at default

238	    \`--surface-tolerance\` (0.55 mm at r = 100, n = 30) and the surface falls to

240	    its polygons and the vertices sit exactly on the ideal surface.
241	  - Detection signals if we go that way: a constant dihedral of exactly 360/n
242	    all the way around a revolution (B-rep tessellation steps vary, and density
243	    changes at seams), and vertices exactly on the circumscribed circle.
244	  - Worth a corpus model or two before deciding anything.

#!/bin/zsh
# Per-model surface-type breakdown: the acceptance corpus for stage-2 guards.
for m in \
  onshape/stepped_block_toroidal_fillets_fine onshape/stepped_block_toroidal_fillets_medium \
  onshape/rounded_cube_10_r2_fine onshape/part_rounded_cube_10_r2_coarse \
  onshape/pipe_elbow_10_medium onshape/nosecone_medium \
  onshape/pill_fine onshape/chamfered_cube_10_c1_medium \
  ccad/generated/cone_cylinder ccad/generated/filleted_cylinder ccad/generated/filleted_pipe \
  nurbs/bspline_wave nurbs/elliptical_arch nurbs/elliptical_cylinder ; do
  b=$(./target/release/dump_regions tests/$m.stl surfaces 2>/dev/null \
      | awk '{for(i=1;i<=NF;i++) if($i=="plane")p++; else if($i=="cyl")c++; else if($i=="cone")k++;
              else if($i=="sphere")s++; else if($i=="torus")t++; else if($i=="bspline")b++}
             END{printf "%d/%d/%d/%d/%d/%d", p+0,c+0,k+0,s+0,t+0,b+0}')
  printf "%-17s %s\n" "$(basename $m | cut -c1-17)" "$b"
done

//! Stage 2: Segmentation & Classification (partition-then-classify)
//!
//! Sub-stage numbering (DEVELOPMENT_PLAN):
//!
//! 2.1: Sharp-edge classification (fit-free) + osculating-jet prior
//!      (\`edges.rs\`).
//! 2.2: Region extraction — connected components over interior edges
//!      (\`regions.rs\`).
//! 2.3: Per-region surface discovery (\`classify.rs\` driver) — whole-region
//!      DOF ladder (2.3(a)) then scoped fit competition (2.3(b), \`compete.rs\`)
//!      with bent-tube/torus promotion (2.3(c)) for the tangent chains the
//!      ladder cannot explain, and B-spline fitting of the leftover components
//!      (2.3(e), \`bspline.rs\`). Whatever remains degrades to per-facet planes.
//! 2.4: Resolution & repair — relabel / merge / re-split + invariants
//!      (\`repair.rs\`).
//! 2.5: Selected-surface output.
//!
//! The analytic fitters (\`planar.rs\`, \`conical.rs\`, \`spherical.rs\`,
//! \`toroidal.rs\`) share the \`surface::SurfaceType\` trait, driven generically
//! by the ladder and competition. The old BFS hypothesis-generation pipeline
//! that predated this rewrite was removed (Rewrite Roadmap step 7).

pub mod edges;
pub mod regions;

mod surface;
pub(crate) mod bspline;
mod classify;
mod compete;
mod conical;
mod cylindrical;
mod planar;
mod repair;
mod spherical;
mod toroidal;

pub use self::bspline::BSplineHypothesis;
pub use self::edges::{EdgeClass, EdgeClassification, JetField};
pub use self::regions::Region;

use crate::config::Config;
use crate::stage1::{ConnectedMesh, MeshFace, MeshVertex};
use crate::viz::{self, VizAction};
use std::time::Instant;
use std::error::Error;
use std::fmt::{Display, Formatter};

// Re-export used by conical.rs via \`super::bounding_box_diagonal\`.
use self::spherical::bounding_box_diagonal;

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Constants
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/// Minimum number of mesh faces required to accept a cylindrical hypothesis.
/// Any real CAD tessellation of a cylinder produces at least 3 facets around
/// the circumference; this rejects spurious 2-face fits (e.g. adjacent torus
/// facets that locally approximate a cylinder).
pub(crate) const MIN_CYLINDER_FACES: usize = 3;

/// Minimum number of mesh faces required to accept a spherical hypothesis.
/// Sphere fitting has 4 degrees of freedom (cx, cy, cz, r), so at least 4
/// non-degenerate faces are needed for a well-determined fit. This also rejects
/// spurious fits from small patches that are consistent with many surface types.
pub(crate) const MIN_SPHERE_FACES: usize = 4;

/// Minimum number of mesh faces required to accept a toroidal hypothesis.
/// A torus has 7 degrees of freedom (center xyz, axis direction 2, R, r),
/// so at least 7 faces are needed for a well-determined fit.
pub(crate) const MIN_TORUS_FACES: usize = 7;

/// Maximum sphere radius as a multiple of the mesh bounding-box diagonal.
/// With solid-angle coverage validation and surface-tolerance validation during
/// BFS, this no longer needs to be tight — those checks prevent pathological
/// growth. A large value serves as a numerical guardrail against degenerate fits.
pub(crate) const MAX_SPHERE_RADIUS_FACTOR: f64 = 1000.0;

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Hypothesis data structures
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/// A planar surface hypothesis fitted to a set of mesh faces.
#[derive(Debug, Clone)]
pub struct PlanarHypothesis {
    /// Unit normal vector pointing outward from the shell/solid.
    pub normal: [f64; 3],
    /// Signed distance from origin to plane along the normal.
    pub distance: f64,
    /// Set of mesh face indices that fit this hypothesis.
    pub faces: Vec<usize>,
    /// Set of mesh vertex indices on this plane.
    pub vertices: Vec<usize>,
    /// Maximum (positive) distance from any vertex to the plane.
    pub error_max: f64,
    /// Minimum (most negative) distance from any vertex to the plane.
    pub error_min: f64,
    /// Sum of absolute vertex-to-plane distances.
    pub error_abs_sum: f64,
}

/// A cylindrical or conical surface hypothesis fitted to a set of mesh faces.
/// Cylinders are represented as cones with half_angle == 0.
#[derive(Debug, Clone)]
pub struct ConicalHypothesis {
    /// The apex of the cone, or a point on the axis for cylinders.
    pub apex: [f64; 3],
    /// Unit direction vector along the cone/cylinder axis.
    pub axis_direction: [f64; 3],
    /// Half-angle of the cone in radians (0 for cylinders).
    pub half_angle: f64,
    /// Cylinder radius (when half_angle == 0). 0 for true cones.
    pub radius_at_apex: f64,
    /// Whether the surface normal points away from the axis (convex=true) or toward it (concave=false).
    pub convex: bool,
    /// Set of mesh face indices that fit this hypothesis.
    pub faces: Vec<usize>,
    /// Set of mesh vertex indices on this surface.
    pub vertices: Vec<usize>,
    /// Maximum absolute distance from any vertex to the surface.
    pub error_max: f64,
```

/// Compute the edge curve for a tangent edge.
///
/// For tangent edges, \`GeomAPI_IntSS\` often fails or produces degenerate results.
/// Instead, construct the curve analytically based on the surface pair types:
/// - Plane-cylinder tangent: line parallel to cylinder axis at the tangent point
/// - Sphere-cylinder tangent: circle arc on the sphere, in a plane perpendicular
///   to the cylinder axis through the sphere center's projection on the axis
/// - Cylinder-cylinder tangent: line parallel to shared axis direction
/// - Plane-sphere tangent: degenerate (single point) — construct line from vertices
fn compute_tangent_edge_curve(
    edge: &mut ReconEdge,
    face_descriptors: &[FaceDescriptor],
    vertices: &[BRepVertex],
    stage2: &Stage2Output,
    config: &Config,
) -> Result<(), String> {
    let [fi0, fi1] = edge.face_indices;
    let ss0 = &stage2.selected_surfaces[face_descriptors[fi0].selected_surface_idx];
    let ss1 = &stage2.selected_surfaces[face_descriptors[fi1].selected_surface_idx];

    // Determine which surface types we have
    let has_sphere_cylinder = matches!(
        (ss0, ss1),
        (SelectedSurface::Spherical(_), SelectedSurface::Conical(_))
            | (SelectedSurface::Conical(_), SelectedSurface::Spherical(_))
    );

    let is_closed_loop = edge.vertex_indices[0] == usize::MAX || edge.vertex_indices[1] == usize::MAX;

    if has_sphere_cylinder {
        // Sphere-cone tangencies are NOT great circles on the sphere (the
        // tangent circle sits at radius R·cos α, offset along the cone axis),
        // so handle them with the generic axis-perpendicular circle
        // construction from mesh boundary vertices.
        let cone_axis = match (ss0, ss1) {
            (SelectedSurface::Conical(c), _) | (_, SelectedSurface::Conical(c))
                if !stage2.conical_hypotheses[*c].is_cylinder() =>
            {
                let h = &stage2.conical_hypotheses[*c];
                Some((h.apex, h.axis_direction))
            }
            _ => None,
        };
        if is_closed_loop {
            if let Some((axis_origin, axis_dir)) = cone_axis {
                return compute_tangent_edge_curve_axis_circle_closed(
                    edge, &axis_origin, &axis_dir, stage2, config,
                );
            }
            return compute_tangent_edge_curve_sphere_cylinder_closed(
                edge, ss0, ss1, stage2, config,
            );
        }
        // Open arcs: the circle is constructed from the sphere geometry and
        // the two endpoint vertices, which works for both cylinders and
        // near-cylindrical cones (e.g. fillets fitted as slight cones).
        let v0 = &vertices[edge.vertex_indices[0]];
        let v1 = &vertices[edge.vertex_indices[1]];
        return compute_tangent_edge_curve_sphere_cylinder(
            edge, v0, v1, ss0, ss1, stage2, config,
        );
    }

    // Check for torus involvement
    let has_torus = matches!(ss0, SelectedSurface::Toroidal(_))
        || matches!(ss1, SelectedSurface::Toroidal(_));

    if has_torus {
        if is_closed_loop {
            return compute_tangent_edge_curve_torus_closed(
                edge, ss0, ss1, stage2, config,
            );
        }
        let v0 = &vertices[edge.vertex_indices[0]];
        let v1 = &vertices[edge.vertex_indices[1]];
        return compute_tangent_edge_curve_torus_arc(
            edge, v0, v1, ss0, ss1, stage2, config,
        );
    }

    // Non-sphere-cylinder, non-torus tangent edges require vertex endpoints
    if is_closed_loop {
        return Err("tangent edge has no vertex endpoints".to_string());
    }
    let v0 = &vertices[edge.vertex_indices[0]];
    let v1 = &vertices[edge.vertex_indices[1]];

    // For plane-cylinder, cylinder-cylinder, and fallback cases: construct a line
```

> Actually, stepping back: for item A, I don't need to construct full curve objects with handles. What I really need is a predicate—given a candidate boundary face, which side of the tangency locus does it fall on? Signed distance to each surface is sufficient for transversal junctions, but at tangent junctions the question becomes degenerate because both surfaces explain the faces nearly equally near the tangency locus, which is exactly where the 2× margin problem occurs. Let me think through what item A concretely requires: the core issue is that boundary faces sit one tessellation row off due to tangent junctions, where faces just past the junction fall within flood tolerance of the wrong surface. The self-calibrating acceptance tolerance helped, and relabeling moves faces by comparing vertex distances with a 2× margin. Item A's proposal is to...
> 
> Computing the tangency locus between two committed surfaces, then reassigning boundary faces based on which side of that locus they fall on—choosing the mesh-edge chain that best follows it. For the stepped_block case with a plane grazing a torus, the tangency locus is a circle at a specific height, and the correct boundary is the mesh-edge chain closest to that circle. The implementation would add a new pass after relabel/merge that builds adjacent surface pairs from face adjacency and computes their tangency loci to drive boundary reassignment. For a tangent pair, I can define a scalar side function that's zero on the tangency locus and has opposite signs on either side of the surfaces—like using signed radial distance minus the tangency radius for a plane-torus case. Then I assign boundary faces by evaluating this function at their centroids, which naturally creates a cut along mesh edges between the two labeled regions.
> 
> The tricky part is that the side function itself depends on fits that might be contaminated by misassigned faces, so this needs to iterate with refitting just like the relabeling step. It should converge because the side function enforces a global geometric constraint rather than relying on local residual comparisons. For the plane-torus case specifically, offsetting the plane moves the intersection point, but the tangency circle—determined by the torus geometry and the plane's normal—stays fixed, so the error doesn't amplify as much as I might initially expect. The tangency locus is fundamentally where the surface normals align — a clean unifying principle that works across all geometry pairs. Rather than tracking zero-crossings, I should parameterize each surface naturally: for a torus, that's the tube angle around the minor circle, and tangency with a plane occurs at a specific angle. I can then classify faces by whether they're on the tangent side or not using this parameter.
> 
> The most robust approach is to leverage surface A's own geometry — find the tangency curve C_A explicitly, then use signed distance along A's surface from that curve to determine which side each face belongs to. For a torus this becomes the sign of the angle difference from the tangency point, derived from how the surface normal component aligns with the plane normal.

> I've identified the continuous metric from the comparison output and found where line 291 captures the worst edge and vertex location details—exactly what I need for the diagnostic dump. Now I'm ready to outline the next steps: starting with snapping shared region boundaries to the mesh, explaining what led me here, and laying out a concrete plan for the first session without diving into implementation yet.
> 
> The key insight is that the tangency machinery and locus constructions already exist in stage3.rs—they produce OCCT handles and work with ReconEdge/BRepVertex—but stage 2 only needs a side test, which is simpler and can stay as plain f64. What I'm actually replacing is the `relabel_boundaries` function with its 2× margin heuristic and 10-round iteration cap. The validation target has its own complications: the medium test's failures stem from missing surfaces and leftover blobs rather than boundary placement issues.
> 
> The core robustness argument is that the tangency locus—where the two fitted normal fields agree—doesn't shift under small fit errors, unlike an intersection curve. A plane offset by δ from the true tangent plane to a torus moves the intersection circle by roughly √(2rδ), but the minimum-distance locus (points where the torus normal aligns with the plane normal) depends only on geometry and the plane's direction, not its offset. So an offset error moves the locus by zero; only a normal-direction error matters, and that causes roughly linear motion.
> 
> The concrete plan is to first reproduce the baseline and capture the 1.6e-1 mm edge boundary metric, then write a diagnostic side-test that classifies each adjacent surface pair as tangent or transversal, computes the locus, and reports how many faces the current labeling has wrong—giving a regression-free measurement before any changes. ## 🤖 Assistant
<!-- msg_011CdUgvVH9xx3yojbz2r5bu -->

Read TODO.md's first item and did enough reconnaissance to plan. Here's where things stand.

## Next up: item A — snap shared region boundaries to mesh-edge chains

TODO.md's plan is A → B → C, and **A** is explicitly "start here": narrow, provenance-independent, and it *removes* a heuristic instead of adding one. Every remaining `--compare` failure is a boundary sitting one tessellation row off, and nothing in the pipeline reasons about where a boundary *should* be — it falls out of flood plus claim resolution. The seam assumption guarantees the correct boundary exists as a mesh-edge chain, so this is selection from a nonempty set, not a search.

Baseline is clean: working tree has only three untracked `tests/downloaded/` files, HEAD is `d045e8a` (docs-only), stage-2 constant count is **35**.

## Four things I found that should shape how you start

**1. Most of the hard geometry already exists — in stage 3.** [stage3.rs:2353](src/stage3.rs:2353) `compute_tangent_edge_curve` already does exactly the closed-form constructions item A calls for, dispatched by surface-type pair: sphere↔cylinder, torus (closed loop and open arc), axis-perpendicular circle for cones, line-from-vertices fallback for plane↔cylinder. And [stage3.rs:1279](src/stage3.rs:1279) `detect_tangency` classifies an edge as tangent by comparing the two *fitted* normal fields at mesh boundary vertices (2° threshold) — it reads nothing but `Stage2Output` and mesh vertices, so that criterion ports into stage 2 essentially unchanged.

The catch: those functions produce OCCT `Geom_Curve` handles and consume `ReconEdge`/`BRepVertex`. Stage 2 doesn't need a trimmed curve — it needs a **side test**, which is strictly weaker and can be plain f64. So read them as a worked reference for the per-pair math, not as code to call.

**2. There's a sharper argument for the minimum-distance locus than "IntSS is fragile."** The locus is *stable* where the intersection is not. For a plane grazing a torus, the intersection circle moves by √(2rδ) under a plane offset error δ — that's the recorded 4.4e-2 mm amplification. The minimum-distance locus is the set of points where the two normal fields agree, which depends on the plane's *direction* and the torus's own geometry but **not on the plane's offset at all**. A δ error moves it by zero to first order; only normal-direction error moves it, and linearly. That's the property that makes A sound, and it generalizes: for every tangent pair the locus is "where n_A ∥ n_B", which is the same predicate `detect_tangency` already evaluates pointwise.

**3. The thing being replaced is `relabel_boundaries`** ([repair.rs:207](src/stage2/repair.rs:207)). Its two retirable constants are visible in the code: the `d_best < d_own * 0.5` anti-flap margin at line 244 and the `0..10` round cap at line 211. Note it already alternates assignment with refit (lines 261+, added 2026-07-26) — so the loop structure snap needs is in place; what changes is the *criterion* from "compare two local residuals" to "which side of a global locus."

**4. The stated validation target won't flip by itself — plan around that.** The one ignored stage-4 test ([stage4_integration.rs:346](tests/stage4_integration.rs:346)) blames coarse sampling: medium is 82 surfaces vs the reference 71, with ~4 fillet cylinders *missing* and ~14 arc facets still per-facet, blocked on `LEFTOVER_BLOB_MAX`. Boundary snapping doesn't create missing surfaces. So use the **continuous** metric instead — `Compare 3.1: edge boundary max dist` ([stage3.rs:340](src/stage3.rs:340)), currently 1.6e-1 mm on medium — and treat "fine stays passing" as the hard constraint.

## Concrete first-session plan

**Step 0 — baseline.** Build release, save `./tmp/probe.sh` output as the diff target, and run the ignored medium test with `--compare` to capture both the 1.6e-1 number and the worst-edge coordinates ([stage3.rs:291](src/stage3.rs:291) prints the worst boundary vertex position, which feeds straight into `dump_regions … at <x> <y> <z>`).

**Step 1 — build it as a diagnostic before it's a behaviour change.** A new `dump_regions` mode that, for each pair of adjacent committed surfaces: classifies tangent vs transversal by the ported normal-agreement test, computes the locus, and reports how many currently-assigned faces sit on the wrong side. This measures the size of the prize with zero regression risk, works on *fine* (which passes) as a correctness check — the answer there should be ≈0 wrong-side faces — and becomes the instrument for judging the real pass later.

**Step 2 — implement `snap_boundaries` as an additive pass in repair.rs**, running after relabel rather than replacing it, alternating with `refit_geometry` the same way relabel does. Keeping both initially isolates snap's effect; probe first, full suite decisive.

**Step 3 — delete the margin and the round cap** only once snap is demonstrably carrying the boundaries, and record in DEVELOPMENT_PLAN what they were for. That's the first movement of the 35-constant headline number.

**Pair coverage to implement first**, driven by what stepped_block actually contains: plane↔torus (the end cap against the perimeter fillet — the exact case that produced 4.4e-2), torus↔cylinder, cylinder↔plane. Anything else falls through to existing relabel behaviour, which keeps the blast radius small.

The one judgment call I'd flag before coding: whether an unhandled or ill-conditioned pair should fall back to relabel silently or refuse to move faces at all. I'd default to falling back, since that makes step 2 strictly additive — but say if you'd rather it be loud.


## 👤 User

I'd rather it be loud for now. We can reconsider if it actually fires in practice. Implement all steps, then commit. If during the process you decide the fundamental approach is flawed or needs significant reworking, stop and let me know.

/// A spherical surface hypothesis fitted to a set of mesh faces.
#[derive(Debug, Clone)]
pub struct SphericalHypothesis {
    /// Center of the sphere.
    pub center: [f64; 3],
    /// Radius of the sphere (always positive).
    pub radius: f64,
    /// Whether the surface normal points away from center (convex) or toward it (concave).
    pub convex: bool,
    /// Set of mesh face indices that fit this hypothesis.
    pub faces: Vec<usize>,
    /// Set of mesh vertex indices on this sphere.

    pub centroid_error_max: f64,
    /// Sum of absolute vertex-to-surface distances.
    pub error_abs_sum: f64,
}

/// A toroidal surface hypothesis fitted to a set of mesh faces.
#[derive(Debug, Clone)]
pub struct ToroidalHypothesis {
    /// Center of the torus (on the axis, in the plane of the major circle).
    pub center: [f64; 3],
    /// Unit direction vector along the torus rotation axis.
    pub axis_direction: [f64; 3],
    /// Distance from center to the tube centerline (major radius R).
    pub major_radius: f64,
    /// Radius of the tube cross-section (minor radius r).
    pub minor_radius: f64,
    /// Whether face normals point away from the tube center (convex=true, outer surface)
    /// or toward the tube center (concave=false, inner surface).
    pub convex: bool,
    /// Set of mesh face indices that fit this hypothesis.
    pub faces: Vec<usize>,
    /// Set of mesh vertex indices on this torus.
    pub vertices: Vec<usize>,
    /// Maximum absolute distance from any vertex to the torus surface.
    pub error_max: f64,
    /// Maximum absolute distance from any face centroid to the torus surface.
    pub centroid_error_max: f64,
    /// Sum of absolute vertex-to-surface distances.
    pub error_abs_sum: f64,
}

/// Identifies a selected surface hypothesis by type and index.
/// \`Conical\` indexes into \`conical_hypotheses\` and covers both cylinders (half_angle≈0) and true cones.
#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy)]
pub enum SelectedSurface {
    Planar(usize),
    /// Index into conical_hypotheses. Covers both cylinders (half_angle≈0) and true cones.
    Conical(usize),
    Spherical(usize),
    Toroidal(usize),
    /// Index into bspline_hypotheses (stage 2.7 NURBS fallback).
    BSpline(usize),
}


// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Stage 2 output
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

/// The output of Stage 2: the mesh with all hypotheses populated and surfaces selected.
///
/// After stage 2.6 (surface selection), the hypothesis vectors contain only the
/// hypotheses that were actually selected. Unselected hypotheses are pruned.
/// Stage 2.1/2.2 results: the fit-free partition of the mesh
/// (DEVELOPMENT_PLAN §2.1–2.2).
#[derive(Debug, Default)]
pub struct Segmentation {
    /// Per-edge sharp/interior classes (stage 2.1(a)).
    pub edge_classes: EdgeClassification,
    /// Per-face osculating-jet prior (stage 2.1(b)), consumed by 2.3 for
    /// effort allocation. Empty when stage 2.1(b) was not run.
    pub jet: Vec<JetField>,
    /// Region id per mesh face (stage 2.2).
    pub face_region: Vec<usize>,
    /// Regions, ordered by smallest member face index (stage 2.2).
    pub regions: Vec<Region>,
}

#[derive(Debug)]
pub struct Stage2Output {
    /// The mesh from stage 1 with per-face hypothesis indices populated.
    pub mesh: ConnectedMesh,
    /// Selected planar hypotheses (after stage 2.6 pruning).
    pub planar_hypotheses: Vec<PlanarHypothesis>,
    /// Selected conical/cylindrical hypotheses (after stage 2.6 pruning).
    /// Cylinders are represented as cones with half_angle == 0.
    pub conical_hypotheses: Vec<ConicalHypothesis>,
    /// Selected spherical hypotheses (after stage 2.6 pruning).
    pub spherical_hypotheses: Vec<SphericalHypothesis>,
    /// Selected toroidal hypotheses (after stage 2.6 pruning).
    pub toroidal_hypotheses: Vec<ToroidalHypothesis>,
    /// B-spline hypotheses fitted by the stage 2.7 NURBS fallback. These own
    /// the actual OCCT surfaces (stage 3 reuses the same objects).
    pub bspline_hypotheses: Vec<BSplineHypothesis>,
    /// Surfaces selected in stage 2.6 for reconstruction. Each face is
    /// covered by exactly one selected surface. Indices point into the
    /// (pruned) hypothesis vectors above.
    pub selected_surfaces: Vec<SelectedSurface>,
    /// The fit-free partition from stages 2.1/2.2 (partition-then-classify
    /// rewrite). Empty (default) in output produced by the nerfed BFS
    /// pipeline.
    pub segmentation: Segmentation,
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Errors
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

#[derive(Debug)]
pub enum Stage2Error {
    Compare(Stage2CompareError),
}

impl Display for Stage2Error {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut Formatter<'_>) -> std::fmt::Result {
        match self {
            Stage2Error::Compare(e) => write!(f, "stage 2 compare: {e}"),
        }
    }
}

> I'm designing a geometry module that computes a side function for pairs of committed surfaces sharing a mesh boundary, where the function determines which side of the locus a point belongs to. For transversal junctions, this side function is naturally available from the surface geometry itself.
> 
> The key insight is that for tangent cases, the residual comparison between signed distances becomes ill-conditioned since both distances are small and comparable, but the geometric locus is still well-defined. Instead of relying on residuals, I need to compute genuine geometric side functions based on each point's position relative to the tangency curve itself.
> 
> For the specific case of a plane tangent to a torus, the tangency locus is determined by where the plane's normal aligns with the torus's normal — which happens at the topmost or bottommost circle of the torus's tube. This gives a circular locus of radius R centered at the torus center offset by ±r along the axis, lying in the plane perpendicular to that axis. the axis at the tangency height. For a concave fillet where a wall meets a floor, the torus sits with its axis vertical and touches the floor along a circle of radius R. For plane-torus tangency in general, the normal must be nearly parallel to the axis, and the locus is a circle of radius R positioned at height c·a ± r along the axis. Now I'm setting up a side function for plane-torus that uses the radial distance from a point to the axis. The normal vector at any point on the torus is determined by the tube angle φ, and at the tangency circle where the torus touches the plane, the normal aligns with the plane's normal (φ = ±90°). This is actually an extremum of the axial coordinate, so the torus surface folds back on itself near the tangency point—on one side of φ = 90°, the surface continues over the top and wraps back down.
> 
> For a physical fillet between a floor and wall, the tube only spans a quarter circle from φ = 90° (floor contact) to φ = 180° (wall contact), and beyond the tangency the torus would dip below the floor. The correct approach is to use the signed distance to the plane as the side function, where faces on the fillet sit above the plane by a height that grows quadratically near the tangency—this is the degenerate case where the fillet's height follows a quadratic profile.
> 
> To avoid the amplification problem this creates, I should switch to using the surface normal instead of position. The torus's normal rotates linearly with arclength at rate 1/r, while its position departs quadratically, so the tangency locus is where the torus's normal becomes parallel to the plane's normal. I can define a side function based on the angle between these normals, signed by the direction of φ, and compute it by finding the tube-angle φ(p) at each point through the fitted torus geometry.
> 
> The key stability advantage: errors in the plane's offset don't affect the alignment angle at all, errors in the plane's normal direction shift it linearly, and errors in the torus's axis direction also shift it linearly. This gives a position error of r·Δ instead of the quadratic √(2rδ) scaling. For mesh faces on the plane itself, φ is still well-defined as long as the point isn't on the axis, so the side function works consistently across the entire mesh. the relationship holds near the tangency circle too—the angular offset from the tangency point scales linearly with the radial distance from the circle's center, with the tube radius as the scaling factor.
> 
> The side function elegantly reduces to just the signed radial distance from the torus axis relative to its major radius, which defines the tangency circle itself. Points outside this circle have positive values, points inside have negative ones, and the stability of this function depends on how accurately the fitted torus's axis position and major radius are determined.
> 
> This principle extends beautifully to any surfaces of revolution sharing a common axis—the typical tangency scenario in CAD like a fillet torus meeting a perpendicular plane or a coaxial cylinder. In all these cases, the tangency locus is a circle about the shared axis, and the side function becomes simply the difference between the point's actual distance from the axis and the reference distance, making it clean and numerically robust.
> 
> For a plane perpendicular to the torus axis, the tangency circle has radius R (where the torus normal aligns with the plane normal at φ = ±90°), and the side function is just ρ − R. For a coaxial cylinder, the tangency occurs at ρ = R ± r depending on whether it's outer or inner tangency, and the side function naturally uses the axial coordinate instead. is bad and h is good. The key insight is that I should use whichever coordinate varies linearly across the separation—the one whose gradient aligns with the tangent direction of the locus curve. More formally, the side function is the projection onto the tangent plane: s(p) = (p − p₀) · t̂, where t̂ is perpendicular to the locus within that plane. For the cylinder-torus case, this means choosing either the radial or axial direction depending on which one captures the linear separation.
> 
> For a plane tangent to a cylinder, the locus is a line parallel to the axis, and I need to find the direction perpendicular to both the axis and the plane normal—that's the coordinate that varies linearly away from the tangency line. When a sphere is tangent to a cylinder or cone, the locus becomes a circle, and I'm working through how the side function behaves in that configuration.
> 
> Now looking at degenerate cases: coaxial cylinders with the same radius collapse into the same surface, giving a circle locus but leaving the fit under-determined. Coplanar planes, a plane tangent to a sphere at a single point, and coaxial tori all trigger the LOUD flag because they're either degenerate or don't provide enough constraint information.
> 
> For non-tangent intersections where surfaces cross transversally, the intersection curve is well-behaved since the crossing angle stays bounded away from zero, and signed distances work naturally as the side function. The relabeling already handles these cases fine because the residuals diverge significantly. The real challenge is the tangent case, though in sharp-edged models the boundary is already a mesh edge that stage 2.1 partitions correctly without needing repair.
> 
> So the design choice is to make snap handle only tangent pairs deliberately and silently, leaving transversal pairs to the existing behavior since they're already handled correctly by the sharp-edge partition. The warnings should only fire when a pair is detected as tangent but we can't construct a locus for it due to the type combination or geometry involved.
> 
> But I need to be more careful about what "loud" means here. The user wants loudness for unhandled or ill-conditioned pairs — that means refusing to move faces and reporting the issue. For tangent pairs that snap takes responsibility for, if the locus fails or is ill-conditioned, I report loudly and don't move anything. Transversal pairs aren't snap's responsibility at all; that's a scope boundary, not a fallback behavior. I'll document this clearly.
> 
> Actually, I should verify whether transversal pairs really are out of scope by looking at a specific failing case — the medium model's edge 168. I need to check what surfaces meet there and whether the junction is tangent or transversal. The diagnostic dump_regions tool should tell me this, so let me use that to inform the design decision.

//! Debug dump for stage 2 (scratch tool, not shipped).
//!
//! \`dump_regions <model.stl> <mode> [args]\`
//!
//! Stage 2.1/2.2 segmentation: \`summary\`, \`singletons\`, \`adj\`, \`leaks\`,
//! \`rdesc <region>\`, \`geom <face>...\`, \`probe <x> <y> <z> [radius]\`, \`jet
//! <face>\`.
//!
//! Stage 2.3 classification (runs the whole of stage 2):
//! - \`surfaces\` — every committed surface, largest area first, with its
//!   parameters, to line up against a reference STEP's surface list.
//! - \`at <x> <y> <z> [radius]\` — which surfaces own the faces near a point;
//!   for tracing a stage-3 compare failure back to stage 2.
//! - \`owner <face>...\` — the surface owning a face and how well its
//!   neighbours' surfaces explain it (the stage-2.4 relabel criterion).

use brepper::config;
use brepper::stage1::ConnectedMesh;
use brepper::stage2::edges::{classify_edges, compute_jet_field, EdgeClass};
use brepper::stage2::regions::extract_regions;
use brepper::stage1;

fn centroid(mesh: &ConnectedMesh, fi: usize) -> [f64; 3] {
    let f = &mesh.faces[fi];
    let vc = f.vertex_count as usize;
    let mut c = [0.0; 3];
    for &vi in &f.vertex_indices[..vc] {
        let v = &mesh.vertices[vi];
        c[0] += v.x;
        c[1] += v.y;
        c[2] += v.z;
    }
    let n = vc as f64;
    [c[0] / n, c[1] / n, c[2] / n]
}

/// Area of a mesh face (triangle, or a quad as two triangles).
fn tri_area(mesh: &ConnectedMesh, fi: usize) -> f64 {
    let f = &mesh.faces[fi];
    let p = |k: usize| {
        let v = &mesh.vertices[f.vertex_indices[k]];
        [v.x, v.y, v.z]
    };
    let tri = |a: [f64; 3], b: [f64; 3], c: [f64; 3]| {
        let u = [b[0] - a[0], b[1] - a[1], b[2] - a[2]];
        let w = [c[0] - a[0], c[1] - a[1], c[2] - a[2]];
        let x = [u[1] * w[2] - u[2] * w[1], u[2] * w[0] - u[0] * w[2], u[0] * w[1] - u[1] * w[0]];
        0.5 * (x[0] * x[0] + x[1] * x[1] + x[2] * x[2]).sqrt()
    };
    let mut a = tri(p(0), p(1), p(2));
    if f.vertex_count == 4 {
        a += tri(p(0), p(2), p(3));
    }
    a
}

/// Distance from a point to a selected surface (analytic types only).
fn dist_to(
    out: &brepper::stage2::Stage2Output,
    sel: &brepper::stage2::SelectedSurface,
    q: [f64; 3],
) -> f64 {
    use brepper::stage2::SelectedSurface as S;
    match sel {
        S::Planar(i) => {
            let h = &out.planar_hypotheses[*i];
            (h.normal[0] * q[0] + h.normal[1] * q[1] + h.normal[2] * q[2] - h.distance).abs()
        }
        S::Conical(i) => {
            let h = &out.conical_hypotheses[*i];
            let d = [q[0] - h.apex[0], q[1] - h.apex[1], q[2] - h.apex[2]];
            let a = h.axis_direction;
            let t = d[0] * a[0] + d[1] * a[1] + d[2] * a[2];
            let perp = [d[0] - t * a[0], d[1] - t * a[1], d[2] - t * a[2]];
            let r = (perp[0].powi(2) + perp[1].powi(2) + perp[2].powi(2)).sqrt();
            if h.half_angle == 0.0 {
                (r - h.radius_at_apex).abs()
            } else {
                (r * h.half_angle.cos() - t * h.half_angle.sin()).abs()
            }
        }
        S::Toroidal(i) => {
            let h = &out.toroidal_hypotheses[*i];
            let d = [q[0] - h.center[0], q[1] - h.center[1], q[2] - h.center[2]];
            let a = h.axis_direction;
            let t = d[0] * a[0] + d[1] * a[1] + d[2] * a[2];
            let perp = [d[0] - t * a[0], d[1] - t * a[1], d[2] - t * a[2]];
            let r = (perp[0].powi(2) + perp[1].powi(2) + perp[2].powi(2)).sqrt();
            (((r - h.major_radius).powi(2) + t * t).sqrt() - h.minor_radius).abs()
        }
        S::Spherical(i) => {
            let h = &out.spherical_hypotheses[*i];
            let d = [q[0] - h.center[0], q[1] - h.center[1], q[2] - h.center[2]];
            ((d[0].powi(2) + d[1].powi(2) + d[2].powi(2)).sqrt() - h.radius).abs()
        }
        S::BSpline(_) => f64::NAN,
    }
}

fn main() {
    let args: Vec<String> = std::env::args().collect();
    let path = &args[1];
    let mode = args.get(2).map(|s| s.as_str()).unwrap_or("summary");
    let config = config::parse_config_from(["brepper", path, "-q"]).unwrap();
    let mesh = stage1::stage1(&config).unwrap();

        "{} faces, {} regions: sharp={} interior={}",
        mesh.faces.len(),
        regions.len(),
        ec.sharp_count,
        ec.interior_count,
    );

    match mode {
        // Regions sorted by size, with bounding boxes of face centroids.
        "summary" => {
            let mut order: Vec<usize> = (0..regions.len()).collect();
```

/// Compare stage 3.1 adjacency graph against reference STEP shape.
///
/// For each ReconEdge, samples mesh boundary vertices along the edge and checks
/// that each is within vertex_tolerance of a STEP edge.
/// For each BRepVertex, checks that it is within vertex_tolerance of a STEP vertex.
fn compare_adjacency_to_step(
    edges: &[ReconEdge],
    vertices: &[BRepVertex],
    stage2: &Stage2Output,
    config: &Config,
) -> Result<(), Stage3CompareError> {
    let mesh = &stage2.mesh;
    let compare_shape = config.compare_shape.as_ref().unwrap();

    // Build compounds of edges and vertices from the reference STEP shape
    let edge_compound = build_edge_compound(compare_shape);
    let vertex_compound = build_vertex_compound(compare_shape);

    // Check ReconEdge boundary vertices against STEP edges
    let mut edge_overall_max = 0.0_f64;
    for (ei, edge) in edges.iter().enumerate() {
        let mut max_dist = 0.0_f64;
        let boundary = &edge.mesh_boundary_vertices;

        // Sample mesh boundary vertices: for short edges check all, for longer ones sample
        let step = if boundary.len() <= 10 { 1 } else { boundary.len() / 10 };
        let mut sample_indices: Vec<usize> = (0..boundary.len()).step_by(step).collect();
        // Always include first and last
        if let Some(&last_idx) = sample_indices.last() {
            if last_idx != boundary.len() - 1 {
                sample_indices.push(boundary.len() - 1);
            }
        }

        let mut worst_vi = usize::MAX;
        for &si in &sample_indices {
            let vi = boundary[si];
            let v = &mesh.vertices[vi];
            let pt = gp::Pnt::new_real3(v.x, v.y, v.z);
            let d = min_distance_to_shape(&pt, edge_compound.as_shape());
            if d > max_dist {
                max_dist = d;
                worst_vi = vi;
            }
        }

        edge_overall_max = edge_overall_max.max(max_dist);
        // Boundary vertices of tangent edges are allowed the chordal surface
        // tolerance: an imprecise tessellation can place the region boundary
        // one row off the tangent junction (see stage3() comment).
        let edge_tol = if edge.tangent {
            config.surface_tolerance_mm
        } else {
            config.vertex_tolerance_mm
        };
        if max_dist > edge_tol {
            if !config.quiet && worst_vi != usize::MAX {
                let v = &mesh.vertices[worst_vi];
                eprintln!(
                    "  Compare 3.1: edge {ei} worst boundary vertex {worst_vi} at ({:.4}, {:.4}, {:.4}), dist {max_dist:.6e}",
                    v.x, v.y, v.z,
                );
            }
            return Err(Stage3CompareError {
                substage: 1,
                check_type: "edge",
                element_index: ei,
                max_distance: max_dist,
                tolerance: edge_tol,
            });
        }
    }

    // Check BRepVertices against STEP vertices. Vertices adjacent to any
    // TANGENT edge get the chordal surface tolerance for the same reason as
    // tangent-edge boundary vertices: when the tessellation lacks a precise
    // point at a corner where surfaces meet tangentially (e.g. triple points
    // of a fillet network), the mesh corner vertex can sit a fraction of a
    // facet off the true corner — harmless, since corner positions get
    // refined against the reconstructed surfaces downstream.
    let mut vert_overall_max = 0.0_f64;
    for (vi, vert) in vertices.iter().enumerate() {
        let pt = gp::Pnt::new_real3(vert.point[0], vert.point[1], vert.point[2]);
        let d = min_distance_to_shape(&pt, vertex_compound.as_shape());

        vert_overall_max = vert_overall_max.max(d);
        let near_tangent = vert
            .adjacent_edges
            .iter()
            .any(|&ei| edges.get(ei).is_some_and(|e| e.tangent));
        let vert_tol = if near_tangent {
            config.surface_tolerance_mm
        } else {
            config.vertex_tolerance_mm
        };
        if d > vert_tol {
            return Err(Stage3CompareError {
                substage: 1,
                check_type: "vertex",
                element_index: vi,
                max_distance: d,
                tolerance: vert_tol,
            });
        }
    }

    if !config.quiet {
        eprintln!(
            "  Compare 3.1: edge boundary max dist {:.6e} mm, vertex max dist {:.6e} mm",
            edge_overall_max, vert_overall_max
        );
    }

    Ok(())
```

> The edge boundary max distance metric confirms the design is working well at 4.363296e-2 mm. Now I'm building a diagnostic module that defines a geometric locus enum to represent where two committed surfaces meet, starting with a circle variant that tracks signed radial distance from an axis.
> 
> I'm refining the approach to unify the variants into just two cases: a radial locus for cylindrical separation and a planar locus that handles both axial and linear cases through a normal vector. The sign convention assigns the positive side to surface B and negative to surface A, with the ordering recorded in the locus definition.
> 
> To avoid manual sign bookkeeping, I'll determine orientation empirically by evaluating the signed distance at deep interior faces of both surfaces—faces far from the boundary. If the mean sign differs between the two surfaces, the orientation is correct; if they match, the locus doesn't actually separate them, which signals an ill-conditioned problem. This self-checking design validates that the computed locus genuinely separates the two supports.
> 
> For tangency pairs, the surfaces' normals must align along the shared boundary. Looking at the fitted types, a torus paired with a plane where the plane normal is parallel to the torus axis becomes a radial locus, and a torus with a cylinder sharing the same axis becomes a planar locus.
> 
> For a torus and coaxial cone, tangency occurs at a specific circle where the torus normal aligns with the cone normal. The cone's normal makes a fixed angle with its axis, and on the torus the normal depends on the tube angle φ, so I need to find where these align to determine the tangency locus. So the signed distance in the meridian plane from the locus point along the direction perpendicular to the locus gives me a unified scalar field that's rotationally symmetric. This single formulation captures both the radial case (where the perpendicular direction is purely radial) and the axial case (where it's purely vertical), so I can represent it as a generalized meridian function with the axis origin, axis direction, locus coordinates, and the perpendicular direction components—which also subsumes the planar case for non-axisymmetric geometries.
> 
> For a plane tangent to a cylinder, the locus becomes a line on the cylinder surface, and the separating direction in the tangent plane is perpendicular to both the cylinder axis and the plane normal. When combining a sphere with a cone or cylinder that share the same axis, the sphere's meridian (a circle in the meridian plane) provides the tangency condition, so the axisymmetric meridian formulation still applies.
> 
> For sphere and plane, tangency gives a single contact point. Sphere and coaxial torus also work with the meridian approach—circle-circle tangency in the meridian plane determines the contact point. Coaxial cylinders are degenerate (same surface). For a cone and plane, if the plane is perpendicular to the axis, they intersect transversally rather than tangentially; if the plane contains the axis, it's tangent to the cone along a generator line.
> 
> So the strategy is: if both surfaces share a common axis (within tolerance), reduce to the meridian half-plane and find where the two meridian curves touch, then construct a `Meridian` locus. Otherwise, if one surface is a plane and the other is a cylinder or cone with axis parallel to the plane, use a `Planar` locus for the line tangency. Anything else gets marked as degenerate.
> 
> For the meridian curves themselves, I'm mapping each surface type to its 2D representation in the (ρ,h) plane: a plane perpendicular to the axis becomes a horizontal line, a cylinder is a vertical line, a cone is a slanted line, a sphere is a semicircle, and a torus is also a circle. Then tangency between two surfaces reduces to tangency between these 2D curves, which I can solve analytically.
> 
> But I'm realizing that line-to-line tangency in 2D is always degenerate unless the lines are parallel and coincident—so distinct planes, cylinders, and cones can't be tangent to each other in the coaxial case. Yet in 3D, a cone and cylinder can touch along a circle, so I need to reconsider what's happening at that contact. Continuing through the remaining cases: line/circle tangency applies to the plane perpendicular to axis with a torus (giving φ*=±90°, ρ*=R), a sphere where the horizontal line's tangency point lies on the axis (a degenerate case I need to flag), a cylinder with a torus (vertical line at ρ=R_cyl tangent to the circle), and a cylinder with a sphere (tangent when R_cyl equals the sphere's radius r, touching at the equator). For circle/circle tangency, two circles are tangent when the distance between centers equals the sum or difference of their radii, with the tangency point lying on the line connecting the centers.

> Great, consistent.

> So `Meridian { origin (a point on the axis), axis, q_star: [rho*, h*], t: [t_rho, t_h] }` and s(p) = (ρ(p) − ρ*)·t_rho + (h(p) − h*)·t_h.

> And the `Planar` variant for plane-tangent-to-cylinder/cone (non-coaxial case): s(p) = (p − p₀)·m̂.

> Now I need to figure out how to detect tangency and identify which case I'm dealing with. The challenge is that a plane doesn't have a natural axis like a surface of revolution does — it's axisymmetric about any axis perpendicular to its normal, which doesn't uniquely define one.
>

For two non-planar surfaces, their axes must be parallel and coincident within tolerance. If one is planar, I use the other's axis and check that the plane's normal aligns with it. Spheres are special since they're axisymmetric about any axis through their center, so the sphere's center must lie on the other surface's axis. Two planes together are degenerate. Two spheres define an axis along the line joining their centers.
